People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. It’s been about two years and three months now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any days when you feel more like your old self and enjoy the things you're doing?\n\nPatient: Not really. Maybe a moment here or there, but… when I look at the garden plans, or quilting, or book club, it just doesn’t catch me anymore. It feels like those things were for the person I was before.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts of your friend or the circumstances surrounding their death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Not all the time on purpose, but things set it off. A crosswalk signal, a truck going by, even one of those walking-tour maps… and then it’s just there again. I can see her stepping off the curb, like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often wish you could have died with your friend?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I don’t think I wish that, exactly. It’s hard to talk about.\n\nDoctor: Have you been pulling away from friends and family since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. Even when I go to the senior center and sit with people I’ve known for years, I don’t feel really with them. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… I haven’t really been okay since my friend was killed. It’s this constant feeling—guilt, fear, anger—and it just hasn’t gone away like people seemed to expect it would.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes. I avoid that intersection now… I’ll take a longer way around if I have to. And I’ve stopped going to most of the tours or safety meetings, because people end up talking about what happened there, and I just… can’t sit through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the moment when your friend was killed?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. It’s not like I’m choosing to think about it. A crosswalk signal, or hearing a truck go by, even seeing one of those walking-tour maps… it just puts it back in my head. Very sharply. I can see my friend stepping off the curb again before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I used to keep busy with things I liked—my garden, quilting with the ladies, theater outings, book club. Now I see them on the calendar and… I just don’t feel much. Like they’re for someone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost pleasure in the things you're still doing, or is it more that you just don't feel like starting them?\n\nPatient: It’s… both, I suppose. Even when I do make myself go, I don’t really enjoy it. I used to plan the garden beds, quilt with the ladies, go to the theater, book club… now I look at those things on the calendar and just feel almost nothing. Like they belong to someone I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really… not the way I used to. I used to look forward to planning the garden beds, quilting with the ladies, the theater, book club. Now I see them written down and I just feel… almost nothing. Like they’re for some other version of me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Since… since she was killed on that walking tour. It’s been about two years and three months now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any days when you feel more like your old self and enjoy the things you're doing?\n\nPatient: Not really. Maybe a moment here or there, but… when I look at the garden plans, or quilting, or book club, it just doesn’t catch me anymore. It feels like those things were for the person I was before.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts of your friend or the circumstances surrounding their death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Not all the time on purpose, but things set it off. A crosswalk signal, a truck going by, even one of those walking-tour maps… and then it’s just there again. I can see her stepping off the curb, like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often wish you could have died with your friend?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I don’t think I wish that, exactly. It’s hard to talk about.\n\nDoctor: Have you been pulling away from friends and family since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. Even when I go to the senior center and sit with people I’ve known for years, I don’t feel really with them. It’s like they’re talking on one side of a window, and I’m on the other.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to find significant or meaningful that you have completely stopped doing since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes. I used to keep busy with things that mattered to me… planning my garden beds, quilting with the ladies, theater trips, book club. I’d look forward to all of it.\n\nNow I see them on the calendar and… nothing, really. It feels like those belonged to someone I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're to blame for your friend's death or that you should have been able to prevent it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I keep thinking if I’d chosen a different way to go, or if I’d made more of a fuss about that crossing… maybe she’d still be here.\n\nAnd then I get angry all over again. At the driver, at the town… all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think you're a bad person because of what happened to your friend?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “bad person.” But… yes, I blame myself. I keep thinking if I’d picked a different route, or pushed harder about that crossing, she might still be alive.\n\nAnd then I get furious again… at the driver, at the town. It just goes round and round.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

At the driver, at the town… all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think you're a bad person because of what happened to your friend?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “bad person.” But… yes, I blame myself. I keep thinking if I’d picked a different route, or pushed harder about that crossing, she might still be alive.\n\nAnd then I get furious again… at the driver, at the town. It just goes round and round.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any activities that you still find yourself looking forward to?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I avoid that intersection now… I’ll take a longer way around if I have to. And I’ve stopped going to most of the tours or safety meetings, because people end up talking about what happened there, and I just… can’t sit through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the moment when your friend was killed?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. It’s not like I’m choosing to think about it. A crosswalk signal, or hearing a truck go by, even seeing one of those walking-tour maps… it just puts it back in my head. Very sharply. I used to keep busy with things I liked—my garden, quilting with the ladies, theater outings, book club. Now I see them on the calendar and… I just don’t feel much. It’s not like I’m choosing to think about it. A crosswalk signal, or hearing a truck go by, even seeing one of those walking-tour maps… it just puts it back in my head. Very sharply. I used to keep busy with things I liked—my garden, quilting with the ladies, theater outings, book club. Now I see them on the calendar and… I just don’t feel much. I used to look forward to planning the garden beds, quilting with the ladies, the theater, book club. Now I see them written down and I just feel… almost nothing. Like they’re for some other version of me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Since… since she was killed on that walking tour. Maybe a moment here or there, but… when I look at the garden plans, or quilting, or book club, it just doesn’t catch me anymore. Like they’re for someone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost pleasure in the things you're still doing, or is it more that you just don't feel like starting them?\n\nPatient: It’s… both, I suppose. Even when I do make myself go, I don’t really enjoy it. I used to plan the garden beds, quilt with the ladies, go to the theater, book club… now I look at those things on the calendar and just feel almost nothing. Like they belong to someone I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really… not the way I used to. I used to look forward to planning the garden beds, quilting with the ladies, the theater, book club. Now I see them written down and I just feel… almost nothing. Like they’re for some other version of me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Since… since she was killed on that walking tour. It’s been about two years and three months now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any days when you feel more like your old self and enjoy the things you're doing?\n\nPatient: Not really. Maybe a moment here or there, but… when I look at the garden plans, or quilting, or book club, it just doesn’t catch me anymore. It feels like those things were for the person I was before.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts of your friend or the circumstances surrounding their death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Not all the time on purpose, but things set it off. A crosswalk signal, a truck going by, even one of those walking-tour maps… and then it’s just there again. I can see her stepping off the curb, like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often wish you could have died with your friend?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I don’t think I wish that, exactly. It’s hard to talk about.\n\nDoctor: Have you been pulling away from friends and family since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. Even when I go to the senior center and sit with people I’ve known for years, I don’t feel really with them. It’s like they’re talking on one side of a window, and I’m on the other.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to find significant or meaningful that you have completely stopped doing since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes. I used to keep busy with things that mattered to me… planning my garden beds, quilting with the ladies, theater trips, book club. I’d look forward to all of it.\n\nNow I see them on the calendar and… nothing, really. It feels like those belonged to someone I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're to blame for your friend's death or that you should have been able to prevent it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I keep thinking if I’d chosen a different way to go, or if I’d made more of a fuss about that crossing… maybe she’d still be here.\n\nAnd then I get angry all over again. At the driver, at the town… all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think you're a bad person because of what happened to your friend?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “bad person.” But… yes, I blame myself. I keep thinking if I’d picked a different route, or pushed harder about that crossing, she might still be alive.\n\nAnd then I get furious again… at the driver, at the town. It just goes round and round.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find yourself looking forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean… those things are still there — the garden plans, quilting, theater, book club. I used to look forward to them.\n\nNow I just see them on the calendar and feel… almost nothing. Like they’re for someone I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think you're worthless or that your life has no meaning since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know. I wouldn’t say I think that exactly.\n\nThings feel pretty empty sometimes, but “worthless” feels like… a big word. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're worthless or that your life has no purpose since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really think of it that way… “worthless” or “no purpose.” It’s more that things feel kind of blank sometimes. I don’t know how else to put it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're a failure because of what happened to your friend?\n\nPatient: Failure… I don’t know. I do keep going over it, though.\n\nLike, if I’d chosen a different route, or made more of a fuss about that crossing… maybe she’d still be here. And then I get angry all over again — at the driver, at the town, all of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I do keep going over it, though.\n\nLike, if I’d chosen a different route, or made more of a fuss about that crossing… maybe she’d still be here. And then I get angry all over again — at the driver, at the town, all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong emotional connection to your friend that has persisted since their death and is interfering with your ability to move on?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… I haven’t really been okay since my friend was killed. It’s this constant feeling—guilt, fear, anger—and it just hasn’t gone away like people seemed to expect it would.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes. I avoid that intersection now… I’ll take a longer way around if I have to. And I’ve stopped going to most of the tours or safety meetings, because people end up talking about what happened there, and I just… can’t sit through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the moment when your friend was killed?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. It’s not like I’m choosing to think about it. A crosswalk signal, or hearing a truck go by, even seeing one of those walking-tour maps… it just puts it back in my head. Very sharply. I can see my friend stepping off the curb again before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I used to keep busy with things I liked—my garden, quilting with the ladies, theater outings, book club. Now I see them on the calendar and… I just don’t feel much. Like they’re for someone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost pleasure in the things you're still doing, or is it more that you just don't feel like starting them?\n\nPatient: It’s… both, I suppose. Even when I do make myself go, I don’t really enjoy it. I used to plan the garden beds, quilt with the ladies, go to the theater, book club… now I look at those things on the calendar and just feel almost nothing. Like they belong to someone I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really… not the way I used to. I used to look forward to planning the garden beds, quilting with the ladies, the theater, book club. Now I see them written down and I just feel… almost nothing. Like they’re for some other version of me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Since… since she was killed on that walking tour. It’s been about two years and three months now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any days when you feel more like your old self and enjoy the things you're doing?\n\nPatient: Not really. Maybe a moment here or there, but… when I look at the garden plans, or quilting, or book club, it just doesn’t catch me anymore. It feels like those things were for the person I was before.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts of your friend or the circumstances surrounding their death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Not all the time on purpose, but things set it off. A crosswalk signal, a truck going by, even one of those walking-tour maps… and then it’s just there again. I can see her stepping off the curb, like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often wish you could have died with your friend?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I don’t think I wish that, exactly. It’s hard to talk about.\n\nDoctor: Have you been pulling away from friends and family since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. Even when I go to the senior center and sit with people I’ve known for years, I don’t feel really with them. It’s like they’re talking on one side of a window, and I’m on the other.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to find significant or meaningful that you have completely stopped doing since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes. I used to keep busy with things that mattered to me… planning my garden beds, quilting with the ladies, theater trips, book club. I’d look forward to all of it.\n\nNow I see them on the calendar and… nothing, really. It feels like those belonged to someone I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're to blame for your friend's death or that you should have been able to prevent it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I keep thinking if I’d chosen a different way to go, or if I’d made more of a fuss about that crossing… maybe she’d still be here.\n\nAnd then I get angry all over again. At the driver, at the town… all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think you're a bad person because of what happened to your friend?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “bad person.” But… yes, I blame myself. I keep thinking if I’d picked a different route, or pushed harder about that crossing, she might still be alive.\n\nAnd then I get furious again… at the driver, at the town. It just goes round and round.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find yourself looking forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean… those things are still there — the garden plans, quilting, theater, book club. I used to look forward to them.\n\nNow I just see them on the calendar and feel… almost nothing. Like they’re for someone I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think you're worthless or that your life has no meaning since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know. I wouldn’t say I think that exactly.\n\nThings feel pretty empty sometimes, but “worthless” feels like… a big word. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're worthless or that your life has no purpose since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really think of it that way… “worthless” or “no purpose.” It’s more that things feel kind of blank sometimes. I don’t know how else to put it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're a failure because of what happened to your friend?\n\nPatient: Failure… I don’t know. I do keep going over it, though.\n\nLike, if I’d chosen a different route, or made more of a fuss about that crossing… maybe she’d still be here. And then I get angry all over again — at the driver, at the town, all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong emotional connection to your friend that has persisted since their death and is interfering with your ability to move on?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I cared about her, of course… but “interfering with my ability to move on” feels hard to answer. I haven’t really thought about it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong yearning or longing for your friend that is still very distressing for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it yearning. I do miss her, but… I don’t know. It’s not something I can really separate out like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
